The result is encouraging. It is also open to more than one explanation. Pupils who attend may have been more motivated before the program began, or their families may have found transport easier to arrange. A comparison of the final scores combines whatever the lessons contributed with differences that existed before anybody entered the extra classroom.","Suppose places are limited and applicants are selected by a fair lottery. Comparing those offered a place with those not offered one would remove an important source of unequal selection. It would not answer every question about the program. Some successful applicants might decline, while some unsuccessful ones could find other tutoring. The comparison would most directly describe the effect of being offered this particular opportunity under these conditions, rather than the effect of making every pupil attend.","The distinction matters for policy. If a school wants to decide whether to offer the same program again, the first question may be the relevant one. If it wants to replace part of the ordinary timetable with compulsory lessons, it needs to consider a different arrangement. Children who volunteer to spend Saturday studying may respond differently from those required to do so. Teachers may also change their methods when an extra activity becomes part of a formal obligation. Expanding a program can change the conditions responsible for its initial appeal.","Examination scores are not the only possible outcome. Families may value supervised study, contact with teachers, or a place where pupils can work without interruption. Others may regard the loss of weekend time as a substantial cost. An evaluation should identify which benefit is being claimed and whose costs are counted. A program could improve average scores while remaining inconvenient for pupils with caring duties. Conversely, a modest score increase might accompany an important improvement in access to quiet study for a group previously excluded from it.","None of these complications makes evaluation pointless. They make the policy question more precise. The school should decide what it wants to learn before selecting the comparison that will appear in its report. It can then state what the findings support, what remains uncertain, and which change in circumstances would require another test. A useful conclusion need not be a verdict on whether extra lessons are inherently good or bad. It may be a careful decision about which offer to continue, for whom, and with what further information."],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The author mainly emphasizes that evaluations of extra lessons should","options":["match their comparisons and conclusions to the particular policy question.","compare only pupils whose families can arrange weekend transport.","replace voluntary lessons whenever lottery evidence becomes available.","treat examination scores as less important than every other possible benefit."],"answer":0,"explanation":"文章从选择差异、机会效应到强制扩展与多种收益，最终要求先明确政策问题，再选择比较并限定结论。","evidence":[1,2,3,4,5],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Vocabulary","stem":"The word \"modest\" in Paragraph 4 most nearly
